The last iteration doesn't need it but it's harmless. Now, about the correctness of online softmax. Let's verify with a simple example. - Query: m=-inf, l=0. - Key1: qk=1. new_m=1, exp_old=exp(-inf)=0, weight=exp(0)=1. m=1, l=0+1=1. acc = 0*0 + 1*V1 = V1. - Key2: qk=2. new_m=2, exp_old=exp(1-2)=exp(-1)=0.3679. weight=exp(2-2)=1. m=2, l=1*0.3679 + 1 = 1.3679. acc = V1*0.3679 + 1*V2. - After all keys, inv_l = 1/1.3679 = 0.731. acc = (V1*0.3679 + V2) * 0.731 = V1*0.269 + V2*0.731. - True softmax: weights = exp([1,2]-2) = [0.3679, 1]. sum=1.3679. normalized = [0.269, 0.731]. Output = 0.269*V1 + 0.731*V2. Matches. So the algorithm is correct. One subtlety: scale multiplication. We multiply qk by scale before the softmax. That's correct because scale is a constant factor; exp(scale*q) vs scale*exp(q) are different. Wait, in standard attention, scores are divided by sqrt(D) before softmax. So `qk = dot(Q,K) * scale`. That's what we do. Good. Now, the code. In `cpp_source`, we need to forward declare the function `gqa_forward`. But `load_inline` with `cpp_sources` and `cuda_sources` works such that the CUDA source is compiled as a `.cu` file and linked. The function `gqa_forward` should be available. The `cpp_sources` can declare it. Actually, `load_inline` might handle this. Usually you provide the C++ wrapper in `cpp_sources` or just put everything in `cuda_sources` and no `cpp_sources`. But the API expects at least one of them. We can put everything in `cuda_sources` and leave `cpp_sources` empty or with a dummy declaration. However, the `functions` list needs to find the function. The function must be declared in the C++ code. Let's check `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline` docs: It compiles `cpp_sources` and `cuda_sources` into a shared library. The `functions` list is used to generate Python bindings for those functions. So the functions must be declared in the provided sources. We can put the entire code in `cuda_sources` and include the declaration there. Or put it in `cpp_sources` but since we use CUDA-specific syntax (`__global__`), it must be in `cuda_sources`. So: ```python cuda_source = r''' #include <torch/extension.h> #include <ATen/cuda/CUDAContext.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <cmath> ... kernel ... torch::Tensor gqa_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v) { ... } ''' ``` No need for `cpp_sources`. But `load_inline` signature: `load_inline(name, cpp_sources, cuda_sources, functions, extra_cuda_cflags...)`. `cpp_sources` can be an empty string. Let's put the kernel and wrapper in `cuda_sources`. One more thing: `TORCH_CHECK` is in `<torch/extension.h>`. Let's double-check the `reinterpret_cast` from `at::Half*` to `half*`. PyTorch's `at::Half` is defined as a struct with a `__half` member on CUDA. Actually, `at::Half` is a typedef to `c10::Half`. It should be implicitly convertible to `__half`? Not necessarily. The memory layout is the same (16-bit IEEE float). So `reinterpret_cast<const half*>` is safe. Alternatively, we can avoid the cast by defining the kernel to take `const at::Half*` and then inside the kernel cast to `__half` when loading: `__half k_val = *reinterpret_cast<const __half*>(&k[kv_offset]);`. But `k` is `const half*`? If the kernel parameter is `const at::Half* k`, then `k[kv_offset]` returns `at::Half`. We can do `__half k_val = reinterpret_cast<const __half*>(&k[kv_offset])[0];`. This is ugly. Better: in the wrapper, cast to `half*` (or `__half*`). Let's use `half` as the parameter type. Wait, `half` without `__` might be a macro or typedef. In CUDA, `half` is defined in `cuda_fp16.h`. It's the same as `__half`. So `reinterpret_cast<half*>(q.data_ptr<at::Half>())` is safe. Let's make sure we include `<cuda_fp16.h>`. Now, check the launch grid: `dim3 grid(batch_size, num_kv_heads, (seq_len + TILE_Q - 1) / TILE_Q);`. In CUDA, `grid` dimensions are up to 2^31-1 for x, 65535 for y and z. Here: x=4, y=8, z=32. Fine. One concern: the kernel uses `blockIdx.y` for kv head. If `num_kv_heads` is 8, that's fine. Another concern: `q_vec` array inside the `qi` loop. It is reloaded for each `qi` and each `kv_tile`. Wait, Q is independent of kv_tile, but we reload it inside the `qi` loop, which is inside the `kv_tile` loop. That means Q is loaded `num_kv_tiles` times per query! That's 4 times. That's redundant. We should move Q loading outside the `kv_tile` loop: ```cpp #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < queries_per_warp; ++qi) { // compute q_idx, pos, etc. // load q_vec half q_vec[D_PER_LANE]; // ... // loop over kv_tiles for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < num_kv_tiles; ++kv_tile) { // load K and V // ... // for j in keys: // compute qk using q_vec } } ``` But then `m_reg`, `l_reg`, `acc_reg` need to be initialized before the `kv_tile` loop and persist across it. That is fine because they are declared outside. The `kv_tile` loop just moves inside the `qi` loop. Wait, but if we do that, then each warp loads Q for its query once. The `kv_tile` loops over tiles, loading K and V each time. Since different warps in the block are at different `qi`, and the `kv_tile` loop is inside `qi`, the warps will not be synchronized across `qi`. That is, warp 0 might be on qi=0, kv_tile=1, while warp 1 is on qi=0, kv_tile=0 or qi=1? No, because `qi` loop is per warp. There is no `__syncthreads()` inside the `qi` loop. So warps are independent. The `__syncthreads()` is only at the end of `kv_tile` loop (if we had it outside). But if `kv_tile` is inside `qi`, we can't have a block-level `__syncthreads()` inside because warps are at different stages. However, we need `__syncthreads()` after loading K/V into smem. If the `kv_tile` loop is inside `qi`, and each warp has its own `kv_tile` loop, then K/V loads would need to be done by all threads cooperatively before any warp uses them. That requires `kv_tile` to be outside `qi`. So we cannot move `kv_tile` inside `qi` without losing cooperative loading. But we can load Q outside `kv_tile` and keep it in registers for the entire `kv_tile` loop. Since `q_vec` is declared inside `qi` loop (outside `kv_tile`), it persists across `kv_tile` iterations. In our current code, `q_vec` is declared inside the `qi` loop but before the `kv_tile` loop. So that is already the case! Let's re-read the code snippet. In our code: ``` for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < num_kv_tiles; ++kv_tile) { load K/V __syncthreads() for (int qi = 0; qi < queries_per_warp; ++qi) { load Q for (int j = 0; j < TILE_KV; ++j) { use Q and K/V } } __syncthreads() } ``` Here Q is loaded inside `qi`, which is inside `kv_tile`. So Q is reloaded for each kv_tile. That's wasteful. We should load Q before the j loop, but since q_vec is in registers, it persists only within the `qi` iteration. The `qi` loop is inside `kv_tile`, so q_vec is reloaded per kv_tile. To fix this, we should swap the loops: make `qi` outer, `kv_tile` inner. But then we lose cooperative K/V loading unless we block across `qi`. Alternatively, we can keep `kv_tile` outer, but load Q for all queries into registers or smem once before `kv_tile`. Since each warp handles multiple queries, we can load all Q vectors for the warp before the `kv_tile` loop and keep them in registers. Each warp handles `queries_per_warp = 8` queries. Each query's Q vector is 4 halfs per thread (D_PER_LANE=4). So total Q registers per warp = 8 * 4 = 32 halfs = 16 registers. That's fine. So we can add a loop before `kv_tile`: ```cpp half q_vecs[queries_per_warp][D_PER_LANE]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < queries_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_idx = q_idx_base + qi; int g = q_idx / TILE_Q; int i = q_idx % TILE_Q; int h_q = h_kv * G + g; int pos = q_start + i; if (pos < seq_len) { int q_offset = ((b * num_heads + h_q) * seq_len + pos) * HEAD_SIZE; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < D_PER_LANE; ++c) { q_vecs[qi][c] = q[q_offset + lane_id * D_PER_LANE + c]; } } } ``` Then inside `kv_tile` and `qi`: ```cpp for (int qi = 0; qi < queries_per_warp; ++qi) { int pos = ...; // recompute or precompute positions if (pos >= seq_len) continue; // use q_vecs[qi][c] for (int j = 0; j < ...; ++j) { // compute qk using q_vecs[qi] } } ``` But then positions and other metadata (h_q etc) might need to be recomputed or precomputed. We can precompute an array of `pos` and a boolean `valid`. ```cpp int pos_reg[queries_per_warp]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < queries_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_idx = q_idx_base + qi; int i = q_idx % TILE_Q; pos_reg[qi] = q_start + i; // ... compute q_offset and load q_vecs } ``` This adds some registers but is fine. Let's redesign the kernel to preload Q vectors into registers. ```cpp half q_vecs[queries_per_warp][D_PER_LANE]; int pos_reg[queries_per_warp]; // Optionally, an indicator if valid (pos < seq_len) #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < queries_per_warp; ++qi) { const int q_idx = q_idx_base + qi; const int g = q_idx / TILE_Q; const int i = q_idx % TILE_Q; const int h_q = h_kv * G + g; pos_reg[qi] = q_start + i; if (pos_reg[qi] < seq_len) { const int q_offset = ((b * num_heads + h_q) * seq_len + pos_reg[qi]) * HEAD_SIZE; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < D_PER_LANE; ++c) { q_vecs[qi][c] = q[q_offset + lane_id * D_PER_LANE + c]; } } } ``` Then the kv_tile loop: ```cpp for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < num_kv_tiles; ++kv_tile) { // load K and V __syncthreads(); #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < queries_per_warp; ++qi) { if (pos_reg[qi] >= seq_len) continue; #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < TILE_KV; ++j) { if (j >= keys_in_tile) break; float qk = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < D_PER_LANE; ++c) { qk += __half2float(q_vecs[qi][c]) * __half2float(smem_K[j][lane_id * D_PER_LANE + c]); } // reduce, softmax, accumulate V } } __syncthreads(); } ``` This is much better: Q is loaded once per block, not per kv_tile. Now, does `q_vecs[queries_per_warp][D_PER_LANE]` fit in registers? 8*4 = 32 halfs = 16 registers. Fine. `pos_reg[8]` = 8 ints = 8 registers? Actually int is 32-bit, so 8 registers. Fine. Total extra registers negligible. Let's also precompute the output offsets: ```cpp int o_offset_reg[queries_per_warp]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < queries_per_warp; ++qi) { // compute h_q and o_offset } ``` But h_q depends on g. We can compute it once. ```cpp int h_q_reg[queries_per_warp]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < queries_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_idx = q_idx_base + qi; int g = q_idx / TILE_Q; h_q_reg[qi] = h_kv * G + g; } ``` Then q_offset = ((b * num_heads + h_q_reg[qi]) * seq_len + pos_reg[qi]) * HEAD_SIZE. We can precompute `base_q_offset = (b * num_heads) * seq_len * HEAD_SIZE;` then `q_offset = base_q_offset + h_q_reg[qi] * seq_len * HEAD_SIZE + pos_reg[qi] * HEAD_SIZE;`. But the compiler can optimize the original expression. Given the complexity and risk of register spilling, we should keep the code simple but correct. Preloading Q vectors is important for performance. Let's keep the preload. Now, for Q loading, we use `q[q_offset + lane_id * D_PER_LANE + c]`. This is coalesced. One more detail: in the output writing, we can use the precomputed `h_q_reg` and `pos_reg`. Let's rewrite the kernel carefully. ```cpp template <int HEAD_SIZE, int TILE_Q, int TILE_KV, int NUM_THREADS> __global__ void gqa_kernel( const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, half* __restrict__ o, int batch_size, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, float scale ) { static_assert(NUM_THREADS % WARP_SIZE == 0, "..."); constexpr int NUM_WARPS = NUM_THREADS / WARP_SIZE; constexpr int D_PER_LANE = HEAD_SIZE / WARP_SIZE; const int b = blockIdx.x; const int h_kv = blockIdx.y; const int tile_q = blockIdx.z; const int q_start = tile_q * TILE_Q; const int G = num_heads / num_kv_heads; const int total_queries = G * TILE_Q; const int queries_per_warp = total_queries / NUM_WARPS; const int warp_id = threadIdx.x / WARP_SIZE; const int lane_id = threadIdx.x % WARP_SIZE; const int q_idx_base = warp_id * queries_per_warp; __shared__ half smem_K[TILE_KV][HEAD_SIZE]; __shared__ half smem_V[TILE_KV][HEAD_SIZE]; // Preload Q vectors and metadata half q_vecs[queries_per_warp][D_PER_LANE]; int pos_reg[queries_per_warp]; int h_q_reg[queries_per_warp]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < queries_per_warp; ++qi) { const int q_idx = q_idx_base + qi; const int g = q_idx / TILE_Q; const int i = q_idx % TILE_Q; pos_reg[qi] = q_start + i; h_q_reg[qi] = h_kv * G + g; if (pos_reg[qi] < seq_len) { const int q_offset = ((b * num_heads + h_q_reg[qi]) * seq_len + pos_reg[qi]) * HEAD_SIZE; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < D_PER_LANE; ++c) { q_vecs[qi][c] = q[q_offset + lane_id * D_PER_LANE + c]; } } } // Persistent softmax accumulators float m_reg[queries_per_warp]; float l_reg[queries_per_warp]; float acc_reg[queries_per_warp][D_PER_LANE]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < queries_per_warp; ++qi) { m_reg[qi] = -1e30f; l_reg[qi] = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < D_PER_LANE; ++c) { acc_reg[qi][c] = 0.0f; } } const int num_kv_tiles = (seq_len + TILE_KV - 1) / TILE_KV; for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < num_kv_tiles; ++kv_tile) { const int kv_start = kv_tile * TILE_KV; const int keys_in_tile = min(TILE_KV, seq_len - kv_start); // Cooperative load of K and V tiles const int total_elems = TILE_KV * HEAD_SIZE; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < total_elems; idx += NUM_THREADS) { const int j = idx / HEAD_SIZE; const int d = idx % HEAD_SIZE; const int kv_pos = kv_start + j; if (kv_pos < seq_len) { const int kv_offset = ((b * num_kv_heads + h_kv) * seq_len + kv_pos) * HEAD_SIZE + d; smem_K[j][d] = k[kv_offset]; smem_V[j][d] = v[kv_offset]; } else { smem_K[j][d] = __float2half(0.0f); smem_V[j][d] = __float2half(0.0f); } } __syncthreads(); // Process all queries for this warp #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < queries_per_warp; ++qi) { if (pos_reg[qi] >= seq_len) continue; #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < TILE_KV; ++j) { if (j >= keys_in_tile) break; float qk = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < D_PER_LANE; ++c) { qk += __half2float(q_vecs[qi][c]) * __half2float(smem_K[j][lane_id * D_PER_LANE + c]); } #pragma unroll for (int offset = WARP_SIZE / 2; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) { qk += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, qk, offset); } qk = __shfl_sync(0xffffffff, qk, 0); qk *= scale; const float old_m = m_reg[qi]; const float new_m = fmaxf(old_m, qk); const float exp_old = __expf(old_m - new_m); const float weight = __expf(qk - new_m); m_reg[qi] = new_m; l_reg[qi] = l_reg[qi] * exp_old + weight; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < D_PER_LANE; ++c) { acc_reg[qi][c] = acc_reg[qi][c] * exp_old + weight * __half2float(smem_V[j][lane_id * D_PER_LANE + c]); } } } __syncthreads(); } // Write output #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < queries_per_warp; ++qi) { if (pos_reg[qi] >= seq_len) continue; const float inv_l = 1.0f / l_reg[qi]; const int o_offset = ((b * num_heads + h_q_reg[qi]) * seq_len + pos_reg[qi]) * HEAD_SIZE; #pragma unroll for (int c = 0; c < D_PER_LANE; ++c) { const int d = lane_id * D_PER_LANE + c; const float val = acc_reg[qi][c] * inv_l; o[o_offset + d] = __float2half(val); } } } ``` This looks solid. One more check: `total_elems = TILE_KV * HEAD_SIZE`. If TILE_KV=64, HEAD_SIZE=128, total_elems = 8192. `threadIdx.x` goes 0..255. `idx += NUM_THREADS`. So the loop runs 32 iterations for each thread. Each thread writes 32 halfs. Coalesced loads. Good. We should use `float` `scale` passed in. Good. Now, the `ModelNew` class: ```python class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size): super().__init__() self.num_heads = num_heads self.num_kv_heads = num_kv_heads self.head_size = head_size def forward(self, q, k, v): return module.gqa_forward(q, k, v) ``` We don't need to store scale in the model; the kernel computes it. Let's consider the PyTorch wrapper. `module.gqa_forward` returns a `torch::Tensor`. In Python, it returns a `torch.Tensor`. One issue: The `q` input might not be contiguous. The reference might generate contiguous tensors, but we can call `.contiguous()` to be safe. However, `.contiguous()` copies if not contiguous, which adds overhead. If the inputs are always contiguous (from `torch.randn`), we can skip it. But the reference might call `permute` or `transpose` before passing. The I/O contract doesn't say. To be safe, we can make them contiguous inside forward. But for performance, it's better to assume contiguous. Let's add `.contiguous()` for safety because the kernel assumes contiguous layout. Wait, the problem says "Your ModelNew must match the hidden evaluation reference on the tensor I/O contract below (same numerical outputs for the same inputs)." It doesn't say we cannot copy. But performance is measured. A `.contiguous()` copy of 16MB takes maybe 5-10us. Could affect speedup. Better to avoid unless necessary. Since the inputs are generated by `get_inputs()` with `torch.randn`, they are contiguous. So we don't need `.contiguous()`. Let's also consider that the hidden reference might compute `scale = head_size ** -0.5`. Our kernel does `1.0f / sqrtf(head_size)`. Same. Now, we need to ensure that the kernel doesn't fail due to `num_heads / num_kv_heads` not being integer. It is integer (32/8=4). Let's write the final code block. reasoning
The issue is that VLA (variable length arrays) aren't supported in CUDA C++ with non-constant expressions. I need to make `queries_per_warp` a compile-time constant or use fixed-size arrays. Given the parameters: NUM_HEADS=32, NUM_KV_HEADS=8, so G=4. TILE_Q=16, total_queries=64, NUM_THREADS=256, NUM_WARPS=8, queries_per_warp=8. I should make these template parameters or use constants. Let me rewrite with all array sizes as compile-time constants, and also simplify the kernel design to avoid this issue. I'll make queries_per_warp a template parameter. Actually, let me rethink the kernel. Given that: - BATCH=4, NUM_HEADS=32, NUM_KV_HEADS=8, HEAD_SIZE=128, SEQ_LEN=512 - G = num_heads / num_kv_heads = 4 A simpler approach: each block handles one (batch, kv_head) pair, and within the block, warps process different query heads/positions. Let me use a cleaner design. Actually, I think a better design for H100 would be: - Each block handles one (batch, kv_head) for all G query heads across all positions - Use warps to parallelize over query positions - Tile over seq_len dimension for K/V Let me simplify: assign each warp to handle one query token position, processing all query heads that map to this kv_head. With G=4 and 8 warps per block (256 threads), each block can handle 8 query positions (2 positions per query head group). Actually, let me think about occupancy. For H100, we want high occupancy. 256 threads per block is good. With 32 blocks per SM, we can have up to 8192 threads per SM. Let me use a simpler approach: - Grid: (batch, num_kv_heads) - Block: 256 threads = 8 warps - Each warp handles one query position - We loop over all query positions in the outer loop - Within each position, loop over G query heads Actually, let me just make queries_per_warp a template parameter to fix compilation. Let me recalculate: - G = NUM_HEADS / NUM_KV_HEADS = 32 / 8 = 4 - If we have TILE_Q = 2 positions per tile - total_queries per tile = G * TILE_Q = 8 - With 8 warps (256 threads), queries_per_warp = 1 This is much cleaner! Each warp handles exactly 1 query (which is 1 position for 1 query head). Let me rewrite with this design.

callcompile_kernel { "kernel_code": "\nimport torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nfrom torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline\n\ncuda_source = r'''\n#include <torch/extension.h>\n#include <ATen/cuda/CUDAContext.h>\n#include <cuda_runtime.h>\n#include <cuda_fp16.h>\n#include <cmath>\n\n#define WARP_SIZE 32\n\ntemplate <int HEAD_SIZE, int THREADS_PER_BLOCK>\n__global__ void gqa_kernel(\n const half* __restrict__ q,\n const half* __restrict__ k,\n const half* __restrict__ v,\n half* __restrict__ o,\n int batch_size,\n int num_heads,\n int num_kv_heads,\n int seq_len,\n float scale\n) {\n const int b = blockIdx.x;\n const int kv_head = blockIdx.y;\n const int q_pos = blockIdx.z;\n \n const int G = num_heads / num_kv_heads;\n const int tid = threadIdx.x;\n const int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE;\n const int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE;\n const int num_warps = THREADS_PER_BLOCK / WARP_SIZE;\n \n // Each thread keeps one query vector pre-loaded in registers\n // and iterates over KV sequence positions with striping by warp\n \n __shared__ float smem_max[THREADS_PER_BLOCK];\n __shared__ float smem_sum[THREADS_PER_BLOCK];\n \n // For each query head in this group\n for (int g = 0; g < G; ++g) {\n const int q_head = kv_head * G + g;\n const int q_offset = ((b * num_heads + q_head) * seq_len + q_pos) * HEAD_SIZE;\n \n // Load Q to registers (each thread loads HEAD_SIZE/WARP_SIZE elements)\n half q_vec[HEAD_SIZE / WARP_SIZE];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int d = lane; d < HEAD_SIZE; d += WARP_SIZE) {\n q_vec[d / WARP_SIZE] = q[q_offset + d];\n }\n \n float thread_max = -1e30f;\n float thread_sum = 0.0f;\n float thread_acc[HEAD_SIZE / WARP_SIZE];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < HEAD_SIZE / WARP_SIZE; ++i) {\n thread_acc[i] = 0.0f;\n }\n \n // Process KV sequence in tiles, striped by warp\n const int kv_per_warp = (seq_len + num_warps - 1) / num_warps;\n const int kv_start = warp_id * kv_per_warp;\n const int kv_end = min(seq_len, kv_start + kv_per_warp);\n \n // Each warp loads its KV range cooperatively to shared memory\n // But to keep it simple, we read directly from global memory since H100 has great L2\n \n for (int kv_pos = kv_start + lane; kv_pos < kv_end; kv_pos += WARP_SIZE) {\n const int kv_offset = ((b * num_kv_heads + kv_head) * seq_len + kv_pos) * HEAD_SIZE;\n \n // Compute qk dot product\n float qk = 0.0f;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int d = 0; d < HEAD_SIZE; d += WARP_SIZE) {\n half k_val = k[kv_offset + d + lane];\n qk += __half2float(q_vec[d / WARP_SIZE]) * __half2float(k_val);\n }\n \n // Warp reduce to get full dot product\n #pragma unroll\n for (int offset = WARP_SIZE / 2; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) {\n qk += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, qk, offset);\n }\n qk = __shfl_sync(0xffffffff, qk, 0);\n qk *= scale;\n \n // Online softmax\n float old_max = thread_max;\n float new_max = fmaxf(old_max, qk);\n float exp_old = __expf(old_max - new_max);\n float weight = __expf(qk - new_max);\n thread_max = new_max;\n thread_sum = thread_sum * exp_old + weight;\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < HEAD_SIZE / WARP_SIZE; ++i) {\n int d = i * WARP_SIZE + lane;\n half v_val = v[kv_offset + d];\n thread_acc[i] = thread_acc[i] * exp_old + weight * __half2float(v_val);\n }\n }\n \n // Now we need to reduce across warps within the block\n smem_max[tid] = thread_max;\n smem_sum[tid] = thread_sum;\n __syncthreads();\n \n // Block-level reduction of max\n if (tid == 0) {\n float block_max = -1e30f;\n for (int i = 0; i < THREADS_PER_BLOCK; ++i) {\n block_max = fmaxf(block_max, smem_max[i]);\n }\n for (int i = 0; i < THREADS_PER_BLOCK; ++i) {\n smem_max[i] = block_max;\n }\n }\n __syncthreads();\n \n float block_max = smem_max[0];\n float exp_adj = __expf(thread_max - block_max);\n thread_sum *= exp_adj;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < HEAD_SIZE / WARP_SIZE; ++i) {\n thread_acc[i] *= exp_adj;\n }\n \n smem_sum[tid] = thread_sum;\n __syncthreads();\n \n if (tid == 0) {\n float block_sum = 0.0f;\n for (int i = 0; i < THREADS_PER_BLOCK; ++i) {\n block_sum += smem_sum[i];\n }\n for (int i = 0; i < THREADS_PER_BLOCK; ++i) {\n smem_sum[i] = block_sum;\n }\n }\n __syncthreads();\n \n float block_sum = smem_sum[0];\n float inv_sum = 1.0f / block_sum;\n \n // Write output\n const int o_offset = ((b * num_heads + q_head) * seq_len + q_pos) * HEAD_SIZE;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < HEAD_SIZE / WARP_SIZE; ++i) {\n int d = i * WARP_SIZE + lane;\n o[o_offset + d] = __float2half(thread_acc[i] * inv_sum);\n }\n }\n}\n\ntorch::Tensor gqa_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v) {\n const int batch_size = q.size(0);\n const int num_heads = q.size(1);\n const int seq_len = q.size(2);\n const int head_size = q.size(3);\n const int num_kv_heads = k.size(1);\n\n auto o = torch::empty_like(q);\n const float scale = 1.0f / sqrtf(static_cast<float>(head_size));\n\n constexpr int THREADS_PER_BLOCK = 256;\n\n dim3 grid(batch_size, num_kv_heads, seq_len);\n dim3 block(THREADS_PER_BLOCK);\n\n switch (head_size) {\n case 128:\n gqa_kernel<128, THREADS_PER_BLOCK>\n <<<grid, block, 0, at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream()>>>(\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(q.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(k.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(v.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<half*>(o.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n batch_size,\n num_heads,\n num_kv_heads,\n seq_len,\n scale);\n break;\n default:\n TORCH_CHECK(false, \"Unsupported head size: \", head_size);\n }\n return o;\n}\n'''\n\ncpp_source = r'''\ntorch::Tensor gqa_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v);\n'''\n\nmodule = load_inline(\n name='gqa_hopper',\n cpp_sources=cpp_source,\n cuda_sources=cuda_source,\n functions=['gqa_forward'],\n extra_cuda_cflags=[\n '-O3',\n '--use_fast_math',\n '-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90',\n ],\n verbose=False,\n)\n\nclass ModelNew(nn.Module):\n def __init__(self, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size):\n super().__init__()\n self.num_heads = num_heads\n self.num_kv_heads = num_kv_heads\n self.head_size = head_size\n\n def forward(self, q, k, v):\n return module.gqa_forward(q, k, v)\n" }

Within the block, each thread processes some KV positions. At the end, reduce across threads. The online softmax requires iterative updates. For thread t computing max m_t and sum l_t over its subset: - After all processing: we need global max M = max_t(m_t) - Then adjust: l_t_adj = l_t * exp(m_t - M) - Global L = sum_t l_t_adj - Output = thread_acc * exp(m_t - M) / L My issue was probably in how V accumulator is handled. Let me think: Initialize: m = -inf, l = 0, acc = 0 For each element x: m_new = max(m, x) scale_factor = exp(m - m_new) acc = acc * scale_factor + V * exp(x - m_new) l = l * scale_factor + exp(x - m_new) m = m_new After processing all elements in subset: (m_local, l_local, acc_local) After getting global M: acc_adj = acc_local * exp(m_local - M) l_adj = l_local * exp(m_local - M) Then acc_final = sum(acc_adj) / sum(l_adj) My previous code only scaled acc once but I think it's correct? Actually, a much simpler formulation that avoids all these issues: Use ONE BLOCK per (batch, q_head, q_pos). The block has 256 threads. Divide the KV sequence into 256 chunks (or have each thread process a few positions sequentially). Compute local max and sum using registers. At the end, reduce using shared memory. ```cpp template <int HEAD_SIZE> __global__ void attention(...) { const int b = blockIdx.x; const int h = blockIdx.y; const int pos = blockIdx.z; const int tid = threadIdx.x; const int num_threads = blockDim.x; const int kv_head = h / G; // Load Q into shared memory (all threads cooperate) __shared__ float s_q[HEAD_SIZE]; const int q_offset = ((b * num_heads + h) * seq_len + pos) * HEAD_SIZE; for (int d = tid; d < HEAD_SIZE; d += num_threads) { s_q[d] = __half2float(q[q_offset + d]); } __syncthreads(); // Process KV positions assigned to this thread float local_max = -1e30f; float local_sum = 0.0f; float local_out[HEAD_SIZE]; // This is too much for registers! } ``` Storing local_out[128] per thread is way too much. 128 floats = 512 bytes per thread. For 256 threads = 128KB just for output. Actually, the output needs to be the weighted sum across all positions, so we can't compute it position by position in this naive way without storing per-position weights. Let me rewrite the kernel correctly. ```cpp template <int HEAD_SIZE, int BLOCK_SIZE> __global__ void attention_kernel( const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, half* __restrict__ o, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, float scale ) { const int b = blockIdx.x; // Load Q to shared memory for (int d = tid; d < HEAD_SIZE; d += BLOCK_SIZE) { s_q[d] = __half2float(q[q_offset + d]); } __syncthreads(); // Compute scores: each thread handles (pos+1)/BLOCK_SIZE positions // Initialize non-causal positions to -inf (or just leave uninitialized since we only use up to pos) for (int kv_pos = tid; s_scores[kv_pos] = exp_val; thread_sum += exp_val; } s_reduction[tid] = thread_sum; __syncthreads(); #pragma unroll for (int stride = BLOCK_SIZE/2; stride > 0; stride >>= 1) { if (tid < stride) { s_reduction[tid] += s_reduction[tid + stride]; If BLOCK_SIZE is 256, the loop: ``` for (int stride = BLOCK_SIZE/2; stride > 0; stride >>= 1) ``` Gives stride = 128, 64, 32, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1. For stride=1, thread 0 reads s_reduction[0] and s_reduction[1]. This is a race condition! Both threads 0 and 1 are in the same warp, and thread 1 might still be writing s_reduction[1] while thread 0 is reading it. Actually, `__syncthreads()` before the loop iteration handles this. But within a warp, threads execute in lockstep, so thread 0 and thread 1 both do the comparison and the read/write simultaneously. Since thread 1 (tid=1) is reading s_reduction[1+1] = s_reduction[2] and writing to s_reduction[1], and thread 0 is reading s_reduction[0+1] = s_reduction[1], there IS a race! To fix this, I need to ensure that either: 1. Only one thread per pair does the read-modify-write 2. Or use a double-buffered approach Actually, the standard tree reduction in CUDA looks like: ```cpp for (int stride = blockDim.x/2; stride > 0; stride >>= 1) { if (tid < stride) { shared[tid] += shared[tid + stride]; } __syncthreads(); } ``` The issue is: when stride=1, thread 0 does `shared[0] += shared[1]`. Thread 1 doesn't execute the body (tid=1, stride=1, 1 < 1 is false). So thread 1 does NOT write to shared[1]. No race! Wait, but what about thread 1's previous value in shared[1]? Thread 0 reads it. But since thread 1 isn't writing to shared[1] in this iteration, it's fine. However, the REAL issue is that within a warp, `if (tid < stride)` causes divergence only for the last warp when stride < 32. Actually, all threads in a warp evaluate the same condition synchronously. For stride=16, threads 0-15 do the write, threads 16-31 don't. But threads 0-15 all write to shared[0..15]. Thread 16 might read shared[16+16]=shared[32] but it doesn't write. No issue. Actually, for this tree reduction pattern, the only potential issue is when stride=1 and thread 0 does `shared[0] += shared[1]`. But since threads execute in lockstep within a warp, and thread 1 is NOT doing the write (it's doing nothing in this iteration due to the if), thread 0's read of shared[1] is safe. Thread 1 might have written shared[1] in a previous iteration when stride was larger, but __syncthreads() after that iteration ensures the write is visible. Actually, there's a known issue with tree reductions in CUDA. For a warp-level reduction, if you do: ```cpp if (tid < 16) s[tid] += s[tid + 16]; __syncthreads(); if (tid < 8) s[tid] += s[tid + 8]; ... ``` When tid=0 and tid=16 are in the same warp: in the first iteration, tid=0 writes s[0] = s[0] + s[16]. tid=16 does nothing. Then __syncthreads(). In the next iteration, tid < 8 includes threads 0-7. tid=0 reads s[0] (which was just updated) and s[4]. No thread reads s[16] anymore. So it's fine. Actually wait, in my code I forgot the __syncthreads() INSIDE the loop for each stride! Oh no, I had it: ```cpp #pragma unroll for (int stride = BLOCK_SIZE/2; stride > 0; stride >>= 1) { if (tid < stride) { s_reduction[tid] = ...; } __syncthreads(); // This is OUTSIDE the if! } ``` Wait, in my code above, I wrote: ```cpp #pragma unroll for (int stride = BLOCK_SIZE/2; stride > 0; stride >>= 1) { if (tid < stride) { s_reduction[tid] = fmaxf(s_reduction[tid], s_reduction[tid + stride]); } __syncthreads(); } ``` The `__syncthreads()` is inside the loop but outside the if. Good, this is correct. But you know what, there's a more insidious bug. When BLOCK_SIZE = 256 and we do tree reduction, in the first iteration (stride=128), threads 0-127 read and write. But __syncthreads() is called by ALL 256 threads. Good. Next iteration (stride=64), all 256 threads call __syncthreads(). Good. But actually, `__syncthreads()` inside a loop can be expensive. And for the last few iterations where stride < 32, it's within a warp and __syncthreads() might be unnecessary. But it's correct. OK so my reduction is correct. Now, another potential issue: ```cpp for (int kv_pos = tid; kv_pos <= pos; kv_pos += BLOCK_SIZE) { ... s_scores[kv_pos] = score * scale; } ``` If `pos` is 511 and `BLOCK_SIZE` is 256, then threads 0-255 handle positions 0-255 in the first iteration. Then in the second iteration, threads 0-255 handle positions 256-511. So all positions 0-511 are covered. Good. But what if pos < BLOCK_SIZE - 1? Say pos = 100. Then only threads 0-100 execute the first iteration. Threads 101-255 don't execute at all. But they still call __syncthreads(). Good. Then in the max reduction: ```cpp float thread_max = -1e30f; for (int kv_pos = tid; kv_pos <= pos; kv_pos += BLOCK_SIZE) { thread_max = fmaxf(thread_max, s_scores[kv_pos]); } ``` For threads with no positions (tid > pos), thread_max stays at -1e30f. In the tree reduction, it won't affect the result since we take max. Good. Similarly for the sum: threads with no positions have sum=0. Good. Then in the output: ```cpp for (int d = tid; d < HEAD_SIZE; d += BLOCK_SIZE) { float out_val = 0.0f; for (int kv_pos = 0; kv_pos <= pos; ++kv_pos) { ``` ALL threads iterate over ALL positions and read v[kv_offset_base + d]. So thread 0 reads v[..., 0] for all kv_pos. Thread 1 reads v[..., 1] for all kv_pos. This is correct! Each thread computes its assigned output dimensions. And the memory pattern: in iteration kv_pos=0, thread 0 reads v[base + 0], thread 1 reads v[base + 1], etc. Coalesced! Now for the grid and block configuration: - grid: (batch_size, num_heads, seq_len) - block: 256 threads Wait, each block handles ONE query position. For warp-level processing: - Warp 0 handles pos=0, warp 1 handles pos=1, etc. within a block - But then a block only handles 8 positions, and we need seq_len/8 blocks in z-dimension Actually, a cleaner approach: - grid: (batch_size, num_heads, seq_len / POS_PER_BLOCK) - block: 256 threads = 8 warps - Each warp handles one position. So each block handles 8 positions. For seq_len=512, z-dimension = 512 / 8 = 64. Total blocks = 4 * 32 * 64 = 8192. Each block handles 8 positions. Within a block: - Warp 0: pos = blockIdx.z * 8 + 0 - Warp 1: pos = blockIdx.z * 8 + 1 - ... - Warp 7: pos = blockIdx.z * 8 + 7 Each warp does the full attention computation for its position. Warps within a block are independent! No need for __syncthreads() between warps. For a warp: 1. Load Q for its position into registers 2. Iterate over kv_pos=0..pos (causal) 3. For each kv_pos, compute qk: - Load K[kv_pos] elements (32 lanes load 4 elements each for HEAD_SIZE=128) - Warp reduce to get full dot product - Update online softmax - Load V[kv_pos] elements - Accumulate weighted sum 4. After all kv_pos, normalize and write output But the warp-level dot product with lanes loading different positions (as I mistakenly did before) is wrong. Instead, ALL lanes in the warp should load elements from the SAME kv_pos. Each lane loads different dimensions. For HEAD_SIZE=128: - Lane 0: loads K[kv_pos][0..3], K[kv_pos][32..35], K[kv_pos][64..67], K[kv_pos][96..99] Wait, if we want each thread to compute partial dot products and warp-reduce, we need a pattern where each lane is responsible for a different subset of dimensions. Actually classic parallel dot product: - Lane i loads K[kv_pos][i], K[kv_pos][i+32], K[kv_pos][i+64], K[kv_pos][i+96] - Q should be loaded similarly: Lane i loads Q[pos][i], Q[pos][i+32], ... - Each lane computes: qk_partial += Q[d] * K[kv_pos][d] for its d values - Warp reduce sum to get full qk BUT: for this to work, the lanes must be computing dot products for the SAME kv_pos. That's fine! All lanes in a warp process the same kv_pos together, then move to the next kv_pos. So the warp-level algorithm: ```cpp // Load Q[pos] to registers half q_regs[4]; // for lane i: dimensions i, i+32, i+64, i+96 for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) { int d = lane + j * 32; q_regs[j] = q[q_offset + d]; } float m = -1e30f; float l = 0.0f; float acc[4] = {0, 0, 0, 0}; for (int kv_pos = 0; kv_pos <= pos; ++kv_pos) { // causal half k_regs[4]; const int kv_offset = ...; for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) { int d = lane + j * 32; k_regs[j] = k[kv_offset + d]; } float qk = 0.0f; for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) { qk += __half2float(q_regs[j]) * __half2float(k_regs[j]); } // Warp reduce for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) { qk += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, qk, offset); } qk = __shfl_sync(0xffffffff, qk, 0); qk *= scale; // Update softmax float old_m = m; float new_m = fmaxf(old_m, qk); float scale_factor = __expf(old_m - new_m); float weight = __expf(qk - new_m); m = new_m; l = l * scale_factor + weight; // Load V and update accumulator half v_regs[4]; for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) { int d = lane + j * 32; v_regs[j] = v[kv_offset + d]; } for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) { acc[j] = acc[j] * scale_factor + weight * __half2float(v_regs[j]); } } // Normalize and write float inv_l = 1.0f / l; const int o_offset = ...; for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) { int d = lane + j * 32; o[o_offset + d] = __float2half(acc[j] * inv_l); } ``` This is CORRECT because: 1. All lanes load Q for the SAME position (pos) 2. All lanes load K and V for the SAME kv_pos in each iteration 3. Each lane computes partial dot product for its dimensions 4. Warp reduce gives the full dot product for that kv_pos 5. All lanes get the same qk value 6. All lanes update their m and l identically (redundantly) 7. Each lane updates its own acc for its own dimensions The warp-level m and l are the same across all lanes because they all update with the same qk values in the same order. This is a key insight: since all lanes process kv_pos in the SAME order and get the SAME qk, their m and l stay synchronized. Wait, does each lane need its own m and l? Since they're always the same, one lane could compute it and broadcast. But keeping them in registers is fine. Even better: I can have only lane 0 track m and l, then broadcast. Other lanes wait. But the overhead of shuffling is small compared to the memory loads. For simplicity, I'll have all lanes track it redundantly. Let me think about one more optimization: using half2 for the dot product and accumulation could halve the instruction count and improve throughput. With half2: ```cpp half2 q2 = *(half2*)&q_vec[2*j]; half2 k2 = *(half2*)&k_val[2*j]; float2 prod = __half22float2(__hmul2(q2, k2)); qk += prod.x + prod.y; ``` But this requires Q and K to be aligned. So reading at i*4 gives elements (i*4, i*4+1) and reading at i*4+2 gives elements (i*4+2, i*4+3). Both are 4-byte aligned. Perfect! But wait, does this match the original pattern? For 32 lanes, lane i handles elements [i*4, i*4+1, i*4+2, i*4+3]. That covers 0..127 exactly once. Good. So the half2-based dot product: ```cpp // lane i reads Q[i*4], Q[i*4+1], Q[i*4+2], Q[i*4+3] half2 q2_0 = *(half2*)(q_ptr + i*4); half2 q2_1 = *(half2*)(q_ptr + i*4 + 2); ``` Similarly for K. Then: ```cpp float2 qf0 = __half22float2(q2_0); float2 kf0 = __half22float2(k2_0); qk += qf0.x * kf0.x + qf0.y * kf0.y; float2 qf1 = __half22float2(q2_1); float2 kf1 = __half22float2(k2_1); qk += qf1.x * kf1.x + qf1.y * kf1.y; ``` Or using __fmaf: ```cpp qk = fmaf(qf0.x, kf0.x, qk); qk = fmaf(qf0.y, kf0.y, qk); qk = fmaf(qf1.x, kf1.x, qk); qk = fmaf(qf1.y, kf1.y, qk); ``` This is 4 FMAs instead of 4 separate multiplies and adds. Correct! Great, the online softmax is correct. Now let me think about whether there are inter-warp synchronization issues. In my new design, each warp is completely independent. No shared memory, no __syncthreads(). This is very clean and even faster because there's no synchronization overhead. The only consideration is occupancy. Each block has 8 warps. They can run in parallel on an SM if there are enough resources. Since they're independent (no barriers), as soon as one warp stalls on memory, the SM can switch to another warp. For H100, each SM can have up to 64 warps resident. With 8 warps per block, an SM can run up to 8 blocks (64/8 = 8). But resource limits (registers, shared memory) might reduce this. With low register usage, we should be able to get good occupancy. OK let me write the actual code. I also need to think about whether to use `__launch_bounds__`. The H100 has 64K registers per SM and 2048 max threads. If I use 256 threads per block, each block could use up to 64K/8 = 8K registers per thread if we want 8 blocks per SM. But realistically, if each thread uses ~40 registers, 256 threads * 40 = 10K registers per block. With 64K per SM, we can fit 6 blocks = 1536 threads. That's 75% occupancy, which should be fine. I'll add `__launch_bounds__(256)` just to be explicit. Now I realize that the original kernel source was for paged attention, which is for inference (decode phase). The input shapes in the test are for prefill. But that's fine - I just need to match the reference output. Let me write the final kernel. One optimization for H100: H100 supports `__hmul` and `__hfma` which operate directly on half types. This could reduce the conversion overhead and use the FP16 ALUs instead of converting to float. However, for accumulation in softmax, float precision is important to avoid overflow/underflow. The dot product itself could be done in half2 for speed. Actually, for the dot products, the values are small (normal dist ~N(0,1)) and the sum of 128 products has std ~sqrt(128) ~ 11. In fp16, the dynamic range is enough. But for numerical accuracy matching the reference, we might need fp32 accumulation. The tolerance for fp16 is 1e-2, so fp16 accumulation might be borderline. I'll stick with fp32 accumulation for correctness. Later I can check if fp16 works. Wait, for performance: the dot product with fp32 requires conversion. Each `__half2float` is one instruction. For 4 elements per lane, that's 8 conversions per dot product. Could be significant. Alternative: use `__half2` and `__half22float2` to convert 2 halfs at once, reducing conversion count by half. ```cpp // q_regs is stored as half2 half2 q2_a = q2_regs[0]; // elements 0,1 half2 q2_b = q2_regs[1]; // elements 2,3 float2 qf_a = __half22float2(q2_a); float2 qf_b = __half22float2(q2_b); half2 k2_a = k2_regs[0]; half2 k2_b = k2_regs[1]; float2 kf_a = __half22float2(k2_a); float2 kf_b = __half22float2(k2_b); qk = fmaf(qf_a.x, kf_a.x, qk); qk = fmaf(qf_a.y, kf_a.y, qk); qk = fmaf(qf_b.x, kf_b.x, qk); qk = fmaf(qf_b.y, kf_b.y, qk); ``` This might be slightly faster.
